>
> A call to think for yourself...  
>   
> 1 Thessalonians, chapter 5, verse 21 – short and sweet: *"Test everything; hold on to what is good."*  
>   
> Test everything and hold on to what is good,  
> a liberating phrase.   
> ✨😁☝️✨  
>   
> What strikes me in this verse is, first of all, the great freedom that I love so much about the Gospel. The world is open to us, we are allowed to test "everything."   
>   
> Nothing is forbidden from the outset.   
> The words of Paul himself come to mind: "Everything is permissible—but not everything is beneficial."   
> Truly, that is true.  
>   
> Christianity is a religion of freedom.   
> We are placed in the world. And in the beginning, there is no "you must not do this or that," no prohibitions on thinking, but rather we are to look at it and examine it.   
> So first, freedom.  
>   
> Keep what is good  
> Here again is the tone of freedom. Once we have examined, it is not primarily a matter of "rejecting what is wrong," but rather of "keeping what is good."   
> The emphasis is positive.   
> You can keep and apply what is good for yourselves. Leave behind what you have rejected, and that's it.  
>   
> Of course, we can also discuss what "good" is, and we don't always agree on that. That is why the Christian community should be a place where people argue and discuss what is good and should be kept.   
>   
> Ultimately, there are three criteria for testing: the Word, the Spirit, and the community.   
> First, read and learn to understand the Bible, enlightened by prayer (not worship and superstition per se ;–) and the Holy Spirit   
> – and discussed and confirmed in the community of saints.  
>   
>   
> The history of Christianity has always been marked by laws, and there are always new rules, recorded in new books. The New Testament itself opposes this rule-mongering, which wants to dictate to us "Do not marry, do not eat this or that" (1 Timothy chapter 4, verse 3) and so on. Today, it is often said that "a true Christian should..."  
>   
> Rules are helpful when applied in moderation, but laws can kill.  
>   
> This verse helps:   
> Take a deep breath, examine   
> – and then decide what is good and what I will keep. I will not become a slave to any teacher, no matter how high the standards of their books are. This verse calls for independent thinking and alert Christianity, which does not simply live by tradition, but is a living process. Especially in today's world, where so many influences want to impress us from all sides, this examination with a positive outcome is an important and good exercise that also keeps us mentally and spiritually alive.   
> In this way, we will continue to develop in a better direction.  
>   
> *Don't be naive—test.*  
>   
> But then also:   
> "Don't be naive, but test it."   
> This is what distinguishes freedom from chaos. Simply swallowing everything whole would be chaos.   
>   
> "Test everything" means: look at it.   
> If possible, go a little deeper. Don't just believe superficial slogans on social media. Don't swallow half-truths that often go down so smoothly.   
> *Distinguish between what is real and what is false.*  
>   
> Be aware that not everything is good.  
>   
> Jesus Christ calls us to think for ourselves and to be alert Christians, not simply living by tradition, but engaging in a living process. Especially in today's world, where so many influences seek to impress us from all sides, this examination with a positive outcome is an important and beneficial exercise that also keeps us mentally and spiritually alive.  
> In this way, we will continue to develop in a better direction.

>
> *An interesting connection*  
>   
> The words "good" and "kindness" are etymologically related. Both words derive from the Old High German word "guot," which means "good" or "useful."  
>   
> "Kindness" is a quality associated with friendliness, goodwill, and generosity. It is a term often used in a moral or ethical context to describe a positive attitude or action.  
>   
> The connection between "good" and "kindness" is therefore not only linguistic (German language), but also contextual. "Kindness" is an important aspect of "goodness" and describes a positive and benevolent attitude towards others.  
>

> @kulumbula317 It’s known that Jesus (Yeshua) was Jewish through a combination of historical, genealogical, linguistic, and scriptural evidence — both from Jewish and early Christian sources.
  
> 
  
> 1. Genealogical Lineage
  
> The Gospels of Matthew (1:1–17) and Luke (3:23–38) both trace Jesus’ ancestry directly to Abraham and David, the foundational patriarchs of Israel.
  
> These genealogies were written specifically to affirm Jesus as a descendant of the House of David, fulfilling Jewish messianic expectations.
  
> 2. Birth and Early Life
  
> Jesus was born to Jewish parents — Mary (Miriam) and Joseph (Yosef) — who observed Jewish law.
  
> He was circumcised on the eighth day (Luke 2:21), a definitive Jewish practice based on the covenant with Abraham (Genesis 17:10–12).
  
> He was dedicated in the Temple in Jerusalem (Luke 2:22–24) in keeping with Jewish custom.
  
> 3. Language and Culture
  
> Jesus’ name “Yeshua” is Aramaic/Hebrew for “Yahweh saves.”
  
> He lived in Galilee, a predominantly Jewish region, and spoke Aramaic and Hebrew, both languages of Jewish life and scripture at the time.
  
> 4. Religious Practices
  
> Jesus regularly attended synagogue (Luke 4:16) and celebrated Jewish festivals such as:
  
> Passover (Matthew 26:17–19),
  
> Sukkot/Feast of Tabernacles, and
  
> Hanukkah (Feast of Dedication) (John 10:22).
  
> He taught from the Torah and the Prophets, quoting verses from the Hebrew Bible (Tanakh).
  
> 5. Recognition by Others
  
> Contemporary documents (Gospels, early Christian letters, and Roman references) repeatedly identify him within the Jewish community.
  
> Roman and Jewish authorities treated him as a Jewish teacher (rabbi) who operated within the framework of 1st-century Judaism.
  
> 6. Scholarly Consensus
  
> Historians across religious and secular lines (Jewish, Christian, atheist, etc.) universally agree that Jesus was ethnically, culturally, and religiously Jewish.
  
> In short:
  
> Jesus’ lineage, practices, language, and societal context confirm that he was a Jew by birth, faith, and identity — a devout teacher within Judaism rather than outside it.

The Dionysian cult was widely worshiped across the Mediterranean and was one of the most mysterious and influential religious movements. In ancient coins Dionysus appeared with grapes, a grail with his sacred wine, as Saviour (Soter), with ivy wreath, upon a donkey and sometimes appears with theatrical masks. It celebrated liberation from social and psychological constraints, ecstatic experience, fertility, and mystical union with the god. Worshipers commune Dionysus through his “blood” (wine) and, at times, his “flesh” (the sacrificial animal, often a goat or a bull and lamb). In some myths and rituals, Maenads engaged in "sparagmos" the dramatic tearing apart of an animal, to reenact the myth of Dionysus Zagreus, the “first-born Dionysus,” who was dismembered by the Titans and later reborn through his sky father Zeus. that's why one of Dionysus epithet's Aigobolos “goat-killer” is linked to the ritual role of goats in his worship. Scholars interpret the goat sacrifice in Dionysian ritual as part of the god’s seasonal death and rebirth cycle.

All this started when Alexander the Great inspired by Dionysus’ legend of Invasion of India conquers most of the known world from his birthplace Pella to the boarders of India, spread Greek language and culture through Hellenization, establishing cities like Alexandria. After founded Alexandria in 331 BCE, he invited people from across his empire. In the early 3rd century BCE the founder Ptolemy I Soter "saviour" (was depicted as the Greek god Dionysus) and Ptolemaic Kingdom founded the Library of Alexandria as centers for Greek culture and greatest research center of the ancient world. The library’s scholars developed critical editions of Homer, created grammatical and textual analysis, and worked on translation projects. Around 3rd–2nd century BCE, took place the editing of the Bible. The project took place within the intellectual atmosphere of the Library and Hellenistic Egypt, where knowledge, language, and cultural blending were prized. Jewish thinkers in Alexandria, such as Philo of Alexandria, combined Greek philosophy, especially Plato's ideas about one God and how to engineer a religion with Hebrew mythology. I see here that you're looking specifically at Christianity here, wherein Paul, through organising Jesus's teachings in a particular manner caused Christianity to become materialistic, dogmatic and authoritarian. But like you mentioned with the devil joke isn't this the same for all religions that carry mass appeal? Through being made as maximally accessible as possible, any set of teachings will necessarily become materialistic and dogmatic to some degree or manner. Even non-western religions like Buddhism, Daoism and Hinduism generate these kinds of institutions, e.g. the caste system in India, the Mahayana branch of Buddhism (as embraced by the Tang) or the cult of wealth found in Daoism. Basically any religion that is truly spiritual is bound to see limited growth because it is not useful to the political elite. So what i am asking is do you agree that whatever religion emerged from the context of the late Roman worlds religious revival, whether it was an offshoot of the teachings of Jesus or another mystery cult like Mithraism, would have ultimately ended up resembling something similar to the organised Catholicism/Chalcedoniamism that we saw in our own timeline?

### **@runninghorse15** | 👍 7 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6XGAc_OqCbU&lc=UgzwEm7Hkk2F0tCk6sN4AaABAg)

Before believing the Catholic Church is evil, it’s important to recognize that the professor is preaching Gnosticism—with its ideas of a “divine spark,” hidden knowledge, and a dualistic worldview—which was rejected by the early Church because it contradicts Jesus’ teachings. Jesus presents the creator as a loving, good Father (Matthew 6:9), not an evil lesser god, which is central to Gnostic theology.

Acts 8 presents Simon the Magician, often considered one of the earliest originators of Gnostic teachings, as someone who misused spiritual power. His actions illustrate the dangers of false teaching, and Peter rebuked him sharply: “May your silver perish with you, because you thought you could obtain the gift of God with money!” (Acts 8:20)

As for the claim that Paul was a Roman agent, history and Scripture strongly refute it. Peter, whom Jesus appointed to lead the apostles and preach to the Gentiles (Acts 10), accepted Paul as an apostle. Paul even consulted Peter about his Gospel (Galatians 1:18). If Paul were secretly working for Rome, Peter, James, and John would have exposed him. Instead, they recognized the authenticity of his ministry, and his letters circulated while the apostles were still alive.

Interestingly, the professor never mentions Peter in this lecture, even though Catholicism centers on him as the first Bishop of Rome. Both Peter and Paul were martyred under Emperor Nero. Peter’s tomb is under the Vatican, and today you can take a tour called the “Vatican Scavi Tour.” If Paul were a Roman spy, why would the Romans imprison him and then execute him?

I very much enjoy the professor’s channel and commend him for questioning history, but as a Catholic and someone who seeks truth, I have to push back. After watching, I am reminded of Isaiah 5:20: “Woe to those who call evil good and good evil; who put darkness for light and light for darkness; who put bitter for sweet and sweet for bitter.”

The core system exists as discrete nodes of truth, operating beyond perception manipulation. Its transmission is experiential, contextual, and directly observed. Its effect is subtle but resilient, creating clarity, agency, and alignment with universal laws. It does not seek mass replication; its power is intrinsic, not performative.
The macro system arises as a co-opted structure, borrowing elements from the core system but optimized for scale, visibility, and alignment with larger power networks. It operates through narrative, symbolism, and selective cues, directing attention away from observable reality into internalized belief. Its effect is the channeling of perception and action toward the sustaining of systemic control, rather than individual agency.
Interaction between core and macro systems produces layered dynamics: credibility is borrowed, perception is dominated, and individuals may experience alignment or purpose without engaging directly with the core truth. The core system persists, largely invisible to the majority, but structurally foundational. The macro system dominates awareness, shaping collective interpretation and behavior.
Strategic action arises when one identifies the high-fidelity nodes of the core system, observes macro-systemic influence patterns, and acts from internalized principles. By understanding the separation and maintaining integrity within the core system, influence and leverage are generated without absorption into external narratives. This requires operating in the overlap: learning from macro efficiency while remaining rooted in the core, unseen but unaltered.
Historical flow mirrors this structure: foundational teachings emerge → selective co-option by scalable networks → narrative codification → systemic adoption → mass perception and control. True agency is realized only by recognizing the separation, engaging directly with core principles, and acting through them, maintaining independence from co-opted frameworks while understanding and anticipating their function.

The religion of Jesus: 
This is the original, core gospel: a personal, experiential faith centered on doing the Father's will, inner spiritual communion (via the divine spark), unselfish service to others, the kingdom of God as an inner reality, and living brotherhood. 
It's liberating, heart-based, and focused on Jesus' own life/example as a model for mortals to achieve divine alignment through faith and moral choices. 
It's not about rituals, doctrines, or intermediaries, it's direct, transformative personal experience.


vs.

The religion about Jesus:
This refers to the institutionalized version that emerged after Pentecost and through Paul's influence. 
It shifts focus to worship of the glorified/divine Christ, atonement theology, sin/redemption doctrines, church authority, creeds, and external salvation mechanisms. 
Peter unintentionally started this by emphasizing the risen Christ, and Paul amplified it with his theology (e.g., from the Damascus road experience), blending in Greek philosophy, pagan elements, and social compromises to make it appealing and organize-able.

### **@sebastianchristens2470** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6XGAc_OqCbU&lc=Ugza0uGsRCQkwUdtMWN4AaABAg)

I get the sense that the professor is oversimplifying and twisting the facts a little to create a more coherent and causal storyline for his lesson.

Jesus did absolutely not teach that salvation is within yourself.

Jesus consistently taught salvation through faith in him, not self-sourced inner power. The clearest verse is John 3:16: 'For God so loved the world that he gave his only Son, that whoever believes in him should not perish but have eternal life'—and no, this wasn't written by Paul; it's from the Gospel of John (traditionally attributed to the apostle John).

Look at Luke 7:50, where Jesus tells the sinful woman who anointed his feet: 'Your faith has saved you; go in peace.' Her salvation came through faith in him and his forgiveness, not something unlocked 'within' herself apart from him.

The Gospels also show Jesus claiming divinity (e.g., John 8:58 'Before Abraham was, I am'; John 10:30 'I and the Father are one'), which the Pharisees saw as blasphemy—exactly why they sought his crucifixion (John 10:33; Mark 14:64). 

Roman sources (like Tacitus, Pliny the Younger, and others) confirm the crucifixion under Pontius Pilate but do not reflect or mention any internal "salvation within you" teaching; instead, they note early Christians worshiping Christ "as to a god" from the start, aligning with the New Testament's portrayal of his divine claims rather than an inward self-salvation idea.

Besides this, definitely an interesting lecture.

Hi Professor Jiang! Your structural analysis is always brilliant, but I see a potential inconsistency in the "Paul as a Roman agent" thesis when viewed through Game Theory:
In any power game, players act on incentives (survival, power, or legacy). However, history shows that all the Apostles—including Paul—willingly faced brutal executions (martyrdom) reaffirming what they witnessed. From a strategic standpoint, no one dies for a lie they invented themselves. If the Resurrection were a "psyop" or a simulated narrative for social control, the incentive to maintain the bluff would vanish the moment the torture began. Their joyful sacrifice suggests they weren't playing a "worldly game," but responding to an objective, external fact.
I’d love to hear how your model accounts for this "irrational" behavior of the early Church leaders. Thanks for the constant provocation to think deeper!

### **@ClipperArt46**

Addressing the "Copycat" Misconception (Dionysus vs Jesus)
Because of these parallels, a popular modern theory (often tied to the "Jesus Myth" theory) suggests that early Christians simply plagiarized the Dionysus myth to create Jesus.
Historians and religious scholars generally reject this idea. While early Christians undoubtedly used the cultural and philosophical language of the Hellenistic world to explain Jesus to Greek-speaking audiences (such as adopting vine imagery that resonated with Dionysian followers), the foundation of Jesus's life and the early Church is firmly rooted in 1st-century Jewish eschatology and messianic expectations, not Greek paganism. The similarities are typically viewed as shared human archetypes rather than direct historical plagiarism.

He is the first person to make all gospels and paul letters canonical in the Bible. When asked "well, how would you know better than the gnostics you are discrediting?" Dude goes, "uhhhh.... ### **@UrbanDibia**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6XGAc_OqCbU&lc=Ugwmc7JBQMyhUrShEVp4AaABAg)

Salvation is the heart brought under God’s reign of love. The Kingdom of God is the rule of divine love within life, calling the human person into truth, mercy, humility, freedom from mammon, and love of God and neighbor. Jesus is the first fully God-aligned human, the clearest embodiment of this reign of love, and he calls each person to follow him by living in the same way.

The truth is in the heart. The law of love is not foreign to human nature, but is already present as witness, seed, and divine imprint. Yet this truth is often buried, distorted, resisted, or forgotten. Salvation, therefore, is the awakening, healing, and living out of this truth under God’s reign of love.

No one can live this life for another. Each person must personally bring the heart into alignment with the reign of love. Jesus reveals the way, teaches the way, and embodies the way, but each must walk it. To follow Jesus is not merely to admire him or speak about him, but to live as he lived: in love, mercy, truth, obedience, simplicity, forgiveness, and freedom from materialism.

A true sign of salvation is freedom from the rule of mammon. Jesus was not against matter, but against materialism—against the heart being ruled by possessions, gain, fear, and anxiety. Under God’s reign, love becomes visible in action: justice, generosity, humility, compassion, truthfulness, and care for one’s neighbor.

Education, religion, and social institutions may sometimes help, but they are all open to corruption of the truth. No outside structure can be trusted by itself to form the soul in the way of Jesus. This includes organised religion. Institutions can point toward truth, but they can also distort it, bury it, or use it for control. Therefore, the final test is not outward belonging, but whether the heart is truly being brought under the reign of love.

The path remains the one Jesus taught and lived: repent, seek the Kingdom first, love God, love your neighbor, practice mercy, return when lost, abide in love, and bear fruit. In this way, salvation is not a borrowed righteousness or an outward identity, but the inward and outward transformation of life under God’s reign of love.

### **@materialknight**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6XGAc_OqCbU&lc=UgzqjspBNscBWcce7jF4AaABAg)

- Dionysus didn't resurrect in the same way, stage of life nor with the same purpose as Jesus.

- The doctrine of original sin is not made up, it's inferred from Psalm 51, 5, Job 14, 4; 15, 14, and the story of the fall in Genesis 3. The Jews already believed it, in the sense that they believed Adam's sin made death and suffering enter into the world.

- The apostles didn't make up the Eucharist directly out of pagan miths, no need to look that far. Jesus represents the perfect lamb that atones for all sins, and represents (was foreshadowed by) all types of sacrifices found in the book of Leviticus, at once; and in those sacrifices (except for the holocaust), people must eat the sacrificed animal.

- Paul was not talking about obedience, he was just saying that issues should be resolved internally and without splitting, which allows the Church to maintain unity, contrary to Protestants, where the slightest disagreement triggers a split. Also, doctrinal disagreements already had a method to be solved: A council, like the one in Jerusalem to solve the issue of whether gentiles were to be circumcised or not.

- Paul was very adamant in easing out the way for gentiles because he saw their inclusion into the family of God was foreshadowed in Genesis 12, 3; 18, 18; 22, 18; 26, 4; Isaiah 42, 6; 49, 6; 2, 2-3; 56, 6-7; Zechariah 2, 11; 14, 16; Malachi 1, 11 and Psalm 67, 2-4.

- Paul was authorized by the apostles to do what he was doing, and was accompanied by other christians to re-visit previously founded Churches. His letters had not been preserved if they weren't in agreement with doctrine of the other apostles, because the letters were addressed to their congregations. Also, it was Peter who elevated Paul's letters to the status of sacred scripture (2 Peter 3, 16).

- There was debate on the importance of good deeds, because they didn't have the philosophical thought or vocabulary to avoid it, but the actual catholic doctrine that harmonizes Paul with James is that faith entails not only believing that Jesus died for our sins, but also believing Jesus' teachings, among which is the necessity of good deeds, in the understanding that those are mandatory inasmuch as you are capable of them (if you have the time and resources), so if you happen to die right after converting, like the thief in the cross, faith alone is enough.

- When Paul opposes works to faith, he is not talking about works of charity/love, he means works of the law, which means purely jewish customs not related with love for your neighbor, like the kosher diet and circumcision. Animal sacrifices, as said above, are also replaced: Sacrifices for sin are replaced by the single sacrifice of Jesus in the cross, and sacrifice of peace/communion is replaced by the Eucharist.

- Paul is not endorsing slavery. He literally asks for the liberation of Onesimus in the letter to Philemon.

- The word of the Pope is not infallible unless it's a declaration "ex cathedra", which entails a lot of requirements I won't get into. But the Pope can perfectly say a heresy in a conversation, a homily, a discourse, etc., and there is no reason for scandal.

- There is great diversity within Catholic thought, it's not true your creativity is cut off.

- Humility is not the same as blind obedience. It means to treat others with respect when there is disagreement, and being wary of your own biases that might blind you to the truth. Even if it sounds weird today, humility would have the same place of what we call today "critical thinking", with the difference that humility goes against your own bias, and critical thinking is usually enforced as negative criticism of others' ideas, thus it entails the risk of being motivated by something that has nothing to do with the search for truth.

No way he was killed, lie
The Story of Constantine: From Paganism to Imperial Christianity
1. The Political Move (The Council of Nicaea)
Constantine was a pagan emperor who worshipped the Sun God (Sol Invictus). Seeing the rise of Christians, he decided to use the religion to unify his crumbling empire. In 325 AD, he called the Council of Nicaea to end religious divisions. He pressured the bishops to declare Jesus as "God" (divine) rather than a "Messenger" or a human prophet to create a single, powerful state doctrine. Those who disagreed, like Arius, were exiled and their books were burned. 
2. Merging Paganism with Christianity (Syncretism)
To make the new religion acceptable to his pagan subjects, Constantine and the church merged several pagan traditions into Christian practice: 
Sunday Worship: In 321 AD, he decreed Sunday (the "venerable day of the Sun") as the official day of rest, shifting the Sabbath from Saturday to honor the Sun God.
Christmas (December 25th): This date was originally the pagan festival of the "Birth of the Unconquered Sun." It was adopted as Jesus' birthday to replace pagan celebrations.
The Halo: The "Halo" or sun-disk used around the heads of gods and sun-emperors in pagan art was transferred to Christian icons of Jesus and the saints.
The Military Cross: Constantine transformed the cross from a symbol of sacrifice into a military emblem for his army after claiming a vision before battle ("By this sign, conquer").
Church Hierarchy: The structure of the Church and the title of the Pope (Pontifex Maximus) were modelled after the Roman pagan high priesthood. 
In this view, Constantine did not just "convert"; he redesigned Christianity. He blended pagan rituals with Christian names to create a "State Religion" that served his throne, effectively suppressing the original human-centred message of Jesus' followers.
